The average of six readings keeps its already proved equivalence.

The variance O(Y²) has the minimal order: if the nonconstant characters modulo Y are killed, h is uniform over its classes, and E h² is at least the mean of the square of the representative of least modulus of each class, ≥(Y²−1)/12. This limits the method of control by second moment and total variation, without giving a barrier for a finer signed arithmetic error. Positive band-limited filters already appear in 146–147; here they act on the dual of the primorial and the variance saving relative to 809 allows crossing the real cutoff D. The remaining interactions at the scale of the sign are still to be controlled.

### The character subtotal of exactly two primes has positive sign

Let R₂(v) be the filtered subtotal of 819 of all characters of reduced denominator pq with p<q primes ≤D(v), summed with their real coefficients in the sieved reading, before normalizing by v². Unconditionally
    v^(3/2)(log v)² R₂(v) → 4 exp(−γ_E) J,     0.00296<J<0.00298.
Thus the integer pairs of the Fourier expansion contribute with the sign opposite to the one required to compensate the constant. They are smaller than the main term, but larger in modulus than the critical scale v⁻². Do not confuse this subtotal with the surviving semiprime integers of 741.

Proof. Write k(x)=K₁(x), I=∫k and b=φ(P)/P. By the Chinese remainder theorem each character of denominator l=pq has coefficient b/((p−1)(q−1)): at each nontrivial prime the ratio with respect to the constant is −1/(p−1). For l/v→t>0 and p,q→∞, the sum over nonzero primitive frequencies of that denominator, applied to W and divided by v^(−3/2)log v (without the coefficient b/φ(l)), converges to Σ_{a≠0} k̂(a/t), Fourier with phase 2π. For fixed a one uses a Riemann sum, eventual coprimality and the multiplier of 819 tending to one because Y/v→0. For 1≤|a|≤l/2 the second difference of 809 gives a majorant O((l/v)²/a²) after normalizing, also with the filter. The convergence is uniform for p/√v,q/√v in compacts of (0,2]. The same majorant and φ(l)≫l allow removing p<ε√v or q<ε√v at cost O(ε²) in the limsup at the scale of the statement, using Σ_{p≤x}p≪x²/log x and b log v=O(1).

With y=p/√v,z=q/√v, the PNT in each variable, D/√v→2, the factor 1/2 for unordered pairs and b log v→2exp(−γ_E) give the announced prefactor and the integral J=∫_[0,2]² Σ_{a≠0}k̂(a/(yz)) dy dz/(yz). The diagonal p=q has limit mass zero on the compacts. Poisson (an absolutely convergent series by the variation of k') and the change t=yz give
    J=∫₀⁴ log(4/t)[Σ_{n≥1} k(nt)−I/t]dt.
Truncate at t≥ε and change x=nt. Since 1≤x≤4, the discrete part integrated against k(x) is Σ_{n≤x/ε} log(4n/x)/n. Subtract (1/2)log²(4/ε) and use the asymptotics of the sums of 1/n and (log n)/n: what remains is γ₁+γ_E log(4/x)−(1/2)log²(4/x), uniformly in x. Here γ₁ is the first Stieltjes constant. To verify the sign, if L(s)=∫k(x)(4/x)^s dx, then
    L(s)=4^s(1−2^(−3/2−s))(1−2^(−5/2−s))/((3/2+s)(5/2+s)),
    J=γ₁ L(0)+γ_E L'(0)−L''(0)/2.
With python-flint, Arb series of order 3 at default precision and acb.stieltjes(1), one obtained [0.00296731723621 +/- 6.42e−15]. The main negative balance of 809 necessarily lies in the characters of at least three primes. What is missing is the signed ceiling of the set of those orders together with the positive subtotal.

### An explicit negative subtotal gathering all orders of a band of primes

Fix an integer k≥2 and exponents 1/(k+1)<a<b<1/k. In 819 sum ALL nonconstant characters whose reduced denominator has all its prime factors in B=(v^a,v^b]. If R_B is this subtotal in the filtered reading, then, with L=log(b/a),
    R_B(v)/a_T(v) → 2exp(−γ_E)[exp(L) Σ_{j=0}^k (−L)^j/j! −1].
The limit is strictly negative for k odd and positive for k even. In particular (a,b)=(13/50,8/25) gives an unconditional negative contribution of main order from an entire band. The alternating direction comes from the Taylor remainder. The selection is by prime support of the denominator: it admits any number of factors inside the band.

Proof. Let b_P=φ(P)/P and b_B=∏_{p∈B}(1−1/p). By CRT factorization, adding the constant mode to the subtotal yields exactly (b_P/b_B) times the sum with coprimality indicator only with respect to B, with the same shift of 819. To compute the main term the shift can be removed: the bound of 819 applies to any bounded indicator. Without shift no n≤4v has k+1 distinct factors in B, since v^((k+1)a)>4v eventually. By exact inclusion-exclusion only j≤k remain in the physical count. Each product d of j factors satisfies d≤v^(kb)=o(v); the sum of W over its multiples equals (1/d)∫W+O(TV(W)), uniformly, with TV(W)≪v^(−5/2)log v. By uniqueness of the products there are at most v^(kb) moduli, so the total error is o(v^(−3/2)log v). The PNT by partial summation gives Σ_B 1/p→L and Σ_B 1/p²→0. Thus the sum of 1/d over j distinct ones tends to L^j/j!, while b_B→exp(−L). Using b_P log v→2exp(−γ_E), ∫W∼a_T(v)log v and subtracting the constant mode proves the formula.

Inclusion-exclusion truncated by size is classical and there are Buchstab antecedents in 392. What is localized here is a precise part of the compensation of 819 whose collective sign is indeed favorable without RH. Its magnitude is only a fraction of the total excess of the constant mode; denominators mixing bands remain pending. The sign of an isolated Fourier order is not inferred from the truncation of the physical count.

### Interactions of all orders containing prescribed fixed primes

Let F be a fixed set of k≥1 distinct primes. In the filtered reading of 819 sum ALL characters whose reduced denominator is divisible by each p∈F, with no restriction on the other factors. For this subtotal U_F one has unconditionally
    U_F(v)/a_T(v) ∼ (−1)^k k! (log v)^(−k) ∏_{p∈F} log(p)/(p−1).
For example, the interactions containing 2 contribute −(log 2)a_T(v)/log v at first order, even though the mode of denominator 2 has been cancelled. Those containing both 2 and 3 contribute +(log 2)(log 3)a_T(v)/(log v)². These sets overlap and their equivalents are not added as if they were disjoint.

Proof. For B⊆F, the projection that removes every character in which some p∈B appears is equivalent in physical space to replacing its local indicators by b_B=∏_{p∈B}(1−1/p). For the requested subtotal alternate those projections with coefficient (−1)^|B|. The cost of removing the shift filter is O_F(v^(−2)/log v) by 819. In projection B every surviving integer is written uniquely as d m, with d composed exclusively of primes of B (including 1) and m free of prime factors ≤D. Put L=log v and truncate d≤L^A. The cost relative to a_T is O_F(L^(1−A/2)+L^(k+1)/v): count at most 4v/d+1 multiples and use Σ_{d>L^A}1/d≤L^(−A/2)∏_{p∈F}(1−p^(−1/2))^(−1). Choose A>2k+6.

In the truncated part, m must be prime except for the semiprimes of d=1: for d≥2, m≤2v<D², and always m≥v/L^A>D. The semiprimes of d=1 cost O(v^(−2)) by 741. The classical PNT with error O_N(x/log^N x) in θ, by partial summation over [v/d,4v/d], replaces the sum over m prime by
    (1/d) ∫ K_v(t) log t / log(t/d) dt
with error O_{N,A}(a_T(v)/(d L^N)) for any fixed N (the piecewise weight has variation ≪v^(−5/2) after extracting log m). The projector B also contributes its factor b_B. Expand the quotient as a geometric series up to degree k in log d/log t. The integrated and summed remainder is O_F(a_T/L^(k+1)), by finiteness of all moments of log d with weight 1/d and log d≤A log L. The truncated moments can be extended to all d with error o_F(a_T/L^k), by the same geometric tail (increase A if necessary).

Under the mass-one weight b_B/d, log d is a sum of independent variables X_p=e_p log p with P(e_p=j)=(1−1/p)p^(−j), p∈B. In the alternating sum of the moments up to degree k every monomial omitting some prime of F disappears. Only the product of the k linear terms survives in degree k, with coefficient (−1)^k k! and means E X_p=log(p)/(p−1). Finally ∫K_v(t)/(log t)^k dt∼a_T(v)/L^k. The equivalent follows. The argument controls mixtures with any number of additional factors, but its error is not uniform as F grows and is larger than the critical scale; it does not allow summing these signs up to the global objective.

### Uniform sign for a growing number of mandatory bands

Let L=log v, H=√L, and k=k(v)≥1 any integer with k log L=o(H). Partition the primes p≤exp(H) into k bands B_i according to (i−1)H/k<log p≤iH/k. In the filtered sieve of 819 sum ALL characters whose denominator touches each of these k bands, admitting any other factor. Then for v sufficiently large the subtotal has strict sign (−1)^k. In absolute value divided by a_T(v) it is at least (1−o(1)) k! (H/(3kL))^k. Here both the number of simultaneous conditions and the number of primes available in each band grow. RH is not presupposed.

To verify uniformity, couple the projections of 852 with independent geometric variables X_p and put X_i=Σ_{p∈B_i} X_p, S=Σ_i X_i. One has E exp(S/H)=O(1): in the geometric product the logarithm costs O(Σ_{p≤exp H}(p^(1/H)−1)/p)=O(1) by p^(1/H)−1≪log p/H and Mertens. Hence P(S>L/4)≪exp(−H/4). Moreover E X_i=H/k+O(1) uniformly by Σ_{p≤x} log p/(p−1)=log x+O(1). In particular each mean is ≥H/(2k) eventually.

For any union B of the bands, in its projection without shifts the d>v^(1/4) cost O(a_T L exp(−H/4)) since the number of multiples of d is ≤4v/d and b_B/d is the law used above. For d≤v^(1/4), m∼v/d≥v^(3/4) and the classical PNT with error O(x exp(−c√log x)) applies to the same partial summation of 852. All constants are absolute, since log(t/d)≥3L/4. The exceptional semiprimes of d=1 and the filter cost O(v^(−2)) per projection. Thus, with error O(a_T exp(−c' H)) for some fixed c'>0 per projection, the latter is
    ∫K_v(t) E[1_{S≤L/4} f_t(Σ_{i:B_i⊂B} X_i)]dt,    f_t(x)=log(t)/(log(t)−x).
Using the common event instead of truncating only the partial sum costs O(a_T P(S>L/4)) by boundedness of f_t on the truncated part.

Alternate the 2^k projections to require presence in ALL bands. On S≤L/4, after extracting (−1)^k the positive mixed difference Δ_{X₁}…Δ_{X_k} f_t(0) appears. By integration of the k-th derivative it is ≥k!∏X_i/(log t)^k, since f_t^(k)(x)≥k!/(log t)^k on that interval. The truncation preserves (1−o(1))∏ E X_i in the product moment: by independence the total moment factorizes, and the tail is ≤E[S^k 1_{S>L/4}]≪(2kH)^k exp(−H/8), using the preceding exponential moment. This error is negligible compared with (H/(2k))^k by k log L=o(H). Since log t≤L+log 4, the declared bound follows, and the alternating error 2^k exp(−c'H) is negligible against it. The orientation comes from the exact mixed difference, without extrapolating a fixed-order expansion.

This result contains interactions of growing order without truncating the additional factors. It leaves out the denominators that omit some mandatory band; the signs of overlapping classes are not summed, nor is the global critical balance obtained.

### Exhaustive partition by occupied bands and cancellation upon summing back

Under the hypotheses of 866, let V_T be the filtered contribution of the denominators that touch EXACTLY the bands T⊆{1,…,k}, leaving free the primes larger than exp H. V_∅ includes the constant mode. Simultaneously for the 2^k patterns, the sign of V_T is (−1)^|T| eventually. Normalizing by a_T(v) (the integral of 688), the signed mass of the odd patterns is −H/L+O(1/L) and that of the even ones is 1+H/L+O(1/L). This includes the previously omitted patterns without counting any denominator twice.

Proof. First project to exclude all bands of the complement C, and then alternate the projections of the bands in T. In the common model of 866 one obtains (−1)^j Δ_T f_t(S_C), j=|T|, S_C=Σ_{i∈C}X_i, Δ_T=∏_{i∈T}(τ_{X_i}−I), always on S≤L/4. The derivative argument of 866 applies at that new base point. Its lower bound for j≥1 is at least (1−o(1)) j!(H/(3kL))^j in units of the integral, uniformly in T: in the tail estimate replace k by j in the power of S and keep H/(2k) as a bound for each mean. For T empty, f_t(S)≥1. The errors per pattern are O(2^j exp(−c'H)), smaller than the respective bounds.

For the aggregated masses, by the derivative formula, on S≤L/4 the sum of the absolute differences of order j is bounded by (4/3)(4S/(3L))^j, using j!Σ_{|T|=j}∏_{i∈T}X_i≤S^j. The orders ≥2 together cost O(S²/L²). The first differences sum to S/log t+O(S²/L²), and the empty term equals 1+S/log t+O(S²/L²). Now E S=H+O(1), E S²=O(H²) and the tail has exponential cost. The errors of all projections sum in modulus to O(3^k exp(−c'H)); still o(1/L). This gives the refined equivalents.

Audit of the global step: for EACH realization and t, the algebraic sum of the models of the patterns is exactly f_t(0)=1 before the common truncation, where the expressions are defined: the operator identity is ∏_i[τ_{X_i}+(I−τ_{X_i})]=I. With the common truncation the result is 1_{S≤L/4}. The same telescoping in the arithmetic projections preserves exactly the original projection with no bands removed. Therefore the whole series of corrections of the model cancels, including the terms of order higher than H/L. The arithmetic residue still contains the original prime defect and the semiprime cost of 741. The relative precision proved for the model is O(exp(−c'H)), against the relative precision v^(−1/2) that the sign requires. Refining only the pattern count or its moments does not reduce that residue.

### Almost logarithmic compression of the outer prime variable, with zero continuous mass

In 368 and 441 one can replace the outer range x^δ (δ fixed) by C₁ log x (log log x)², with ONE fixed test f that detects each outer zero. The absolute transfer error is O(x^(−1)) and the continuous integral disappears exactly. The compression is only of the index q of Λ, not of the Möbius data defining its weights.

In the construction of 386 take lengths h_j=1/(j log²(j+1)), summable, and density g in the logarithmic variable. The density exists by convolution with the first box; differentiating the first n boxes as measures of variation 2/h_j and using the ceiling 1/h_(n+1) of the next one gives ||g^(n)||∞≤(C n log²(n+2))^n for n≥1, adjusting C. Continuity of each derivative is obtained by leaving two more boxes undifferentiated. For f(y)=g(log y), the identity y^n f^(n)=∏_{j=0}^{n−1}(∂_log−j)g preserves that ceiling with another C: the derivatives of order i≤n are bounded by C₀(Cn log²(n+2))^i and one sums the absolute coefficients of the product. The same holds for f(y)log y by the compact support [1,C₂] and the product rule in the logarithmic variable. Smoothness at both edges by zero extension. The Mellin product of the boxes for f converges without vanishing in Re s>0 as in 386.

Integrating the ordinary Fourier transform by parts n times and choosing n=⌊c|ξ|/log²(2+|ξ|)⌋ with c small, both transforms decay as O(exp(−c'|ξ|/log²(2+|ξ|))). For the sum over frequencies kY, k≥1, the ceiling is preserved with a smaller constant c', for example using log²(2+kY)≪√k log²(2+Y). By Poisson, the error of the factorial quadrature of 382 for Y=x/d is O(Y log(2Y) exp(−c''Y/log²(2+Y))). Fix J=⌊x/[B log x (log log x)²]⌋, corrections of 441 with m=0 and d≤e²J. Then Y≥(B/e²)log x(log log x)², and taking the constant B sufficiently large the exponential error is ≤x^(−5) uniformly. Even summing over all d with the coefficients O(log² x) the cost is O(x^(−1)).

Consequently U(x)−xf̂(1)=Σ_{q≤C₂x/J}Λ(q)g_J(q/x)+O(x^(−1)), since ∫g_J=0 and the support is cut by the exact inversion of 368. A floor −O_ε(x^(1/2+ε)) for that sum, for each ε>0, is equivalent to RH by the same Mellin/Landau argument and the explicit formula under RH for the fixed smooth test. It remains to be proved. The earlier uses of Gevrey and of factorial inversion are explicit antecedents; the saving here comes from quantifying the regularity of an almost analytic compact test, while preserving both its zero-free Mellin and the exact centering.

### The same defect in a growing number of disjoint prime intervals

With the FIXED test of 890, L=log x and R=B L(log L)² for a sufficiently large constant B, uniformly for integer √x≤J≤x/R,
    Σ_{x/(JR)<q≤C₂ x/J} Λ(q) g_J(q/x) = U(x)−x f̂(1)+O(x^(−1)).
The zero-mass corrections of 441 are kept. The lower band can be cut with absolute error, also when J varies with x.

Proof: for q≤x/(JR), f(q/x)=0 and for all d≤e²J the inverse spacing Y=x/(dq)≥R/e². Poisson for Σ_j f(j/Y) gives Y∫f+O(Y x^(−6)) by 890, increasing B. The main term of these sums vanishes by Σλ_d/d=0. The remainder satisfies |g_J(q/x)|≪x^(−5)(log x)^3/q, since |λ_d|≪log²x and Σ_{d≤e²J}1/d≪log x. Summing with Λ(q) costs only O(log² x) more by elementary comparison. The upper factorial transfer of 890 is uniform in these J by the same minimum of Y with q=1 and by the uniform bound on the coefficients of 441. The constant in the final error is independent of the band.

In particular take t_i=R(4C₂R)^i, J_i=⌊x/t_i⌋ for i≥0 and t_i≤√x/2. There are (1/2+o(1)) log x/log log x choices. Since t_i≤x/J_i≤2t_i, the upper end of band i is ≤2C₂t_i, and the lower end of the next one is ≥4C₂t_i. They are disjoint and each sum approximates the SAME number with the indicated uniform error. Therefore it suffices to prove that for each sufficiently large x AT LEAST ONE of these bands has floor −O_ε(x^(1/2+ε)), with constant uniform in x for each ε>0. One can even choose the band after observing the data. An outer zero producing a larger negative excursion forces the simultaneous deficit in all of them. No probabilistic independence is deduced: all bands reuse prefixes of the same μ in their weights. Without a floor for at least one band at each scale, the global condition remains open.

### Replicas with disjoint intervals in BOTH indices and two linking moments

In 900 one can achieve simultaneously disjointness of the bands of q and of the Möbius indices d that are read term by term. For a replica with cut J put A=⌊J/(C₂R)⌋. The only information needed from d≤A is the two numbers
    H₀(A)=Σ_{d≤A} μ(d)/d,     H₁(A)=Σ_{d≤A} μ(d)log(d)/d.
With them and the μ(d) for A<d≤J the defect of 900 is reproduced with absolute error O(x⁻¹), uniform in J. The additional coefficients in J<d≤e²J are two constants computable from those data.

Indeed use h(Y)=Σ_{j≥1}f(j/Y)−Y∫f. In the whole band of q of 900, q/x≤C₂/J<1, so f(q/x)=0. The harmonic condition of 441 gives exactly g_J(q/x)=−Σ_{d≤e²J}λ_d h(x/(dq)). For d≤A and q≤C₂x/J one has Y≥R. By 890, |h(Y)|≪Y x^(−6), so the block d≤A can be OMITTED at absolute cost O(x^(−5)log³ x) after summing with Λ(q), using |μ|≤1. In the two equations of 441 for m=0, the prefixes enter only through L(1)=0 and L'(1)=1, that is Σλ_d/d=0 and Σλ_d log(d)/d=−1. The parts before A are precisely H₀(A),H₁(A). The matrix of the two constants is invertible because the harmonic averages of log d on the two disjoint blocks are different.

In the packing of 900 now use t_i=R(8e²C₂R)^i, t_i≤√x/2. There are still (1/2+o(1))log x/log log x replicas. One has e²J_{i+1}<A_i eventually (by J_i≥x/(2t_i)), in addition to the previous disjointness in q. Rectangles disjoint in both projections are obtained for the variable computation, with two cumulative linking moments per cut. Those two numbers depend on A_i; they are neither universal constants nor independent random variables. The reduction is a control of data dependence, not a new bound on the defect. In particular, to obtain the global floor by induction one would have to control the linking moments together with the signed local contribution.

### Bilinear rectangles without any linking moment, for a fixed detector of the global sign

Here the statistic is CHANGED. For the f of 890 take w=(1+y∂_y)²f. Its Mellin W(s)=(1−s)²f̂(s) only adds a zero at 1, and in particular W(ρ)≠0 at each outer zero. Moreover ∫w=∫w log y=0. Let V(x)=Σ_nΛ(n)w(n/x). The floor V(x)≥−O_ε(x^(1/2+ε)) for all ε>0 is equivalent to RH: under RH the explicit formula and the vertical decay give O(√x) (the main term vanishes), and without RH the Mellin transform on x≥1 retains the nonreal pole of −(ζ'/ζ)W at each outer zero, being regular on the corresponding real segment. Landau applied after adding Cx^(1/2+ε) contradicts the floor for ε<Reρ−1/2.

With R and C₂ of 900 (increase B if needed), A=⌊J/(C₂R)⌋ and √x≤J≤x/R, one has uniformly
    V(x)=−Σ_{x/(JR)<q≤C₂x/J} Λ(q) Σ_{A<d≤J} μ(d) h_w(x/(dq)) + O(x^(−1)),
    h_w(Y)=Σ_{j≥1} w(j/Y).
Neither H₀,H₁ nor corrective coefficients intervene. To verify it, subtract from w(q/x) the sum Σ_{d≤J}μ(d)Σ_j w(djq/x). The difference vanishes exactly for q>C₂x/J because all dj in the support are ≤J and Σ_{d|h}μ(d)=1_{h=1}. Weighting by Λ, the subtracted part is Σ_{d≤J}μ(d)Σ_m log m w(dm/x) by the divisor identity. Its quadrature integral is zero for each d by the TWO moments of w. The estimates of 890 remain valid for w and w log y: two additional logarithmic derivatives in the order-n bound only introduce a polynomial factor in n and log(n+2) absorbable into C^n. The total factorial error is O(x^(−1)). In the remaining part q≤C₂x/J one has w(q/x)=0. Since ∫w=0, Poisson gives |h_w(Y)|≪Y x^(−6) if Y≥R. This removes both q≤x/(JR) and d≤A with total error O(x^(−5)log³x) by harmonic summation in both indices. This time the main term of each d cancels separately.

Use the packing of 910 with t_i≤√x/(10C₂R) instead of √x/2. It keeps (1/2+o(1))log x/log log x replicas and even guarantees that EVERY q used lies below EVERY d used, in addition to the disjointness between intervals of the same type. In this way each replica reads only the individual values of μ and Λ on its own intervals. The values are the actual arithmetic ones and disjointness does not imply independence. The cancellation of the type I terms by means of two moments already appears in 195 (index 35322); the result here is the simultaneous family of replicas without shared data at absolute precision, preserving a fixed detector of all outer zeros. The signed lower bound for some replica per scale remains open.

### Pure Möbius replicas on two disjoint intervals, with polylogarithmic cofactor

With w,C₂,R of 920 (increase B), put t=x/J, A=⌊J/(C₂R)⌋ and E=⌊t/R²⌋. For R²≤t≤√x/(10C₂R) one has uniformly
    V(x)=Σ_{A<d≤J} Σ_{E<e≤C₂t} μ(d)μ(e) log(e)
             Σ_{t/R<ek≤C₂t, k≥1} h_w(x/(dek)) + O(x^(−1)).
In particular k≤C₂R². No values of Λ are used, nor prefixes of μ. The bilinear formula in two μ has antecedents in 195 (index 36232–234); here one proves numerous replicas of the SAME observable with separated support in both indices and a short cofactor.

Quadrature lemma: for R²≤Y≤x, Σ_{m,n≥1}w(mn/Y)=O(x^(−5)), choosing B sufficiently large. Take M=√(C₂Y) and a smooth plateau χ equal to 1 up to 1 and zero from 2 on, with the derivative bounds of 890 (integrate a normalized affine copy of f to obtain the transition). On the support of the product 1=χ(m/M)+χ(n/M)−χ(m/M)χ(n/M) holds, since both indices cannot exceed M. The two simple terms cost O(x^(−6)) when summing first in the free index: ∫w=0 and Y/m≥Y/(2M)≫R for m≤2M, with the constant factor absorbed by choosing B. For the double term, in coordinates s=m/M, r=n/M, the weight χ(s)χ(r)w(C₂sr) is compact inside [1/(2C₂),2]² and satisfies in each variable separately the derivative bounds of 890 uniformly in the other (Leibniz product and bounded dilations). Successive Poisson replaces the double sum by its integral with error O(x^(−6)); the polynomial factors in x are absorbed by increasing B. The integral is zero: for 0<z≤1, ∫χ(s)χ(z/s)ds/s=log(1/z)+2∫₁²χ(s)ds/s, splitting into s<z, z≤s≤1 and s>1. Putting z=sr, the continuous term integrates w(C₂z) against an affine function of log z, and both moments vanish. This proves the lemma.

In the complete residual of 920, before cutting q,d, the sum over multiples of e≤E is exactly
    Σ_n w(en/x) − Σ_{d≤J}μ(d)Σ_{m,n}w(demn/x).
Here x/(de)≥R². By simple Poisson and the lemma each reading of multiples costs O(x^(−4)), uniformly in e. Thus one can subtract from Λ(q) the function −Σ_{e|q,e≤E}μ(e)log e, at total cost O(x^(−2)). The identity Λ=−(μ log)*1 leaves only e>E. The same q,d as in 920 are cut: the modified weight is bounded in modulus by τ(q)log q≤O(x log x) on q≤C₂t, and the Poisson tail removed there admits any fixed power by increasing B. For q>C₂t one first uses the exact vanishing of the complete residual. Substituting q=ek gives the formula and the positive sign in front of μ(d)μ(e).

To obtain simultaneous disjointness choose nominal t equal to R²(8C₂R²)^i up to √x/(20C₂R), and J integer by rounding down x/t; the effective t lies between the nominal one and its double. The intervals of e are disjoint by that separation factor, as are those of d. All the e lie below all the d. Thus there are (1/4+o(1))log x/log log x replicas reading μ on mutually disjoint index sets. Only the one-sided floor of one per x is missing; disjointness of the inputs does not by itself give signed cancellation.

### An outer zero would force almost extreme Möbius excursions at all intermediate scales simultaneously

Let Θ be the supremum of the real parts of the nontrivial zeros. If Θ>1/2, for each 0<δ<1/2 and η>0 there exist arbitrarily large x such that, for ALL real y∈[x^δ,x^(1−δ)], there is an interval of integers I⊂[y/(log x)^4, 2(C₂+1)y] with
    |Σ_{n∈I} μ(n)| ≥ y^(Θ−η).
One can require I to be contained in a single dyadic band (absorbing a logarithmic loss). It is not necessary that there exist a zero with real part exactly Θ.

Proof. In 931 fix J,t and denote by D_J,E_t the maxima of the moduli of sums of μ over subintervals of (A,J] and (E,C₂t], respectively. Double summation by parts gives
    |V(x)| ≤ O((log x)^C) D_J E_t + O(x^(−1))
with C absolute for the fixed test. Indeed, with the cofactor k≤C₂R² fixed, the cut t/R<ek≤C₂t only restricts the interval of e. In coordinates log d,log e the function log(e) h_w(x/(dek)) and its partial derivatives up to one per variable are O(log x): h_w and its first two logarithmic derivatives are globally bounded by Poisson and ∫w=0 (for small Y they vanish). The logarithmic lengths are O(log R). Integrating by parts in both variables costs at most O(log x (1+log R)²) per k, including the edges. The computation of 931 also applies up to t≈√x without the additional separation between ALL rectangles: that separation was used only in the final packing, not in the identities, which require J≥√x and t≥R².

For any sufficiently small ε>0 the fixed detector of 920 has |V(x)|>x^(Θ−ε) arbitrarily far out by Landau and a zero with abscissa >Θ−ε, choosing ε<Θ−1/2. The classical bound M(z)=O_ε(z^(Θ+ε)) (trivial if Θ=1) controls E_t≪t^(Θ+ε) and D_J≪J^(Θ+ε). Since Jt=x, substituting each bound separately into the bilinear inequality yields
    D_J ≫ J^Θ x^(−2ε)/(log x)^C,   E_t ≫ t^Θ x^(−2ε)/(log x)^C
SIMULTANEOUSLY for each allowed J at those same x. If y≤√x choose J=⌈x/y⌉ and use E_t; if y>√x choose J=⌈y⌉ and use D_J. The endpoints of those intervals fall into the claimed ones because R²=o((log x)^4). With 2ε<δη and a strict margin, constants, polylogarithms and the partition into O(log log x) dyadic bands are absorbed. The individual estimate M(z) at exponent Θ and the bilinear method are classical; the additional point is the uniform quantifier for all scales and within one and the same excursion of V.

Sufficient consequence: to rule out a Θ>1/2 it would suffice to have a fixed saving against that exponent in ALL subsums of one of those intermediate strips for each large x. The existence of such a quiet strip does not follow from the PNT nor from the disjointness of indices. RH has not been proved.

### Mertens exponent in EACH prescribed band of almost logarithmic multiplicative width

There exists a fixed constant K>0 such that for y→∞, putting B_y=[y/(K log y (log log y)²),y] and M_osc(B_y)=max_{I⊆B_y interval}|Σ_{n∈I}μ(n)|, one has unconditionally
    log(1+M_osc(B_y))/log y → Θ.
Antecedent 133 proves this limit on [y^(1/(1+h)),y] for fixed h>0. Here the band is narrower and can still be prescribed BEFORE searching for the witness x. In particular RH is equivalent to the existence of ONE sequence y_n→∞ with M_osc(B_{y_n})≤y_n^(1/2+o(1)).

Proof of the change of quantifiers. For any ε>0, in each [y^(7/5),y^(8/5)] with y sufficiently large there is x with |V(x)|≥x^(Θ−ε). Indeed, the explicit formula for the smooth compact test of 920 gives V(exp u)=−Σ_ρ W(ρ)exp(ρu)+O(1) for large u (vanishing main term, bounded trivial zeros; equivalently shift to a fixed negative line). The coefficients are absolutely summable, conjugate in pairs and none vanishes. If Θ>1/2 apply to the series normalized by exp(u/2) the Gaussian lemma of 115 around (3/2)log y with fixed relative width sufficiently small depending on ε, inside the prescribed band. Choose d<Θ−1/2 so close to the supremum and the width so small that d divided by one plus the width is >Θ−1/2−ε. It is not required that the supremum be attained. If Θ=1/2, the normalized series is an absolutely convergent trigonometric series and has positive mean square over intervals of growing length, uniformly in the origin, grouping frequencies and truncating as in 133. Thus it also gives |V|≫√x in that same band.

Now fix J=⌊y⌋, independently of which x is found. For all such x one has √x≤J and t=x/J lies between positive powers of y and below √x/(10C₂R) eventually, so that 931 and the double Abel of 946 apply. Use the classical bound E_t≪_ε t^(Θ+ε) on the opposite factor. One gets D_J≫_ε J^Θ x^(−2ε)/(log x)^C for fixed C. The lower endpoint of its band (A,J] satisfies n>A⇒n>J/(C₂R); since log x≤(8/5)log y, K can be chosen independent of ε and of x so as to include it in B_y. Consequently the liminf is ≥Θ upon letting ε→0; the classical global bound for M gives the converse limsup. Under RH this last bound gives the ceiling of exponent 1/2 along any sequence. For the converse a single sequence suffices by the proved limit.

The methodological consequence is precise: if Θ>1/2, there are no quiet exceptions even when y is chosen freely in the bands B_y. The arithmetic exhibition of ONE quiet sequence at exponent 1/2 remains open. The theorem does not determine the value of Θ.

### Exponent of the mean Mertens dispersion, even after removing exceptions

Increase the constant K of 962 and keep B_y. With M(t)=Σ_{n≤t}μ(n), for every fixed p>0 one has
    lim_{y→∞} log(1+inf_c [(1/y)∫_{B_y}|M(t)−c|^p dt]^(1/p))/log y = Θ.
One can also remove from B_y any measurable set of length ≤y^(1−κ), for any fixed κ>0, chosen after observing M. The center c is free in each band. Thus a single sequence with mean dispersion of exponent at most 1/2, even trimmed in that way, is equivalent to RH.

Proof of the L¹ floor. Take x of 962, L=log x and R=B L(log L)². For each c write E=(1/y)∫_{B_y}|M−c|. Choose J by rounding down a point of [y/2,y] where |M−c|≤3E. Independently choose a by rounding down a point of [y/(8C₂R),y/(4C₂R)] where |M−c|≪R E. Both strips lie in B_y upon increasing K, even after rounding, and M does not change upon rounding. In 931 one can take a instead of A since a≤A and the additional block a<d≤A was removed with absolute error by Poisson. The uniform error for (a,J] is kept. This gives freedom to control the two boundary terms without using the maximum of M.

In the double Abel of 946 fix the cofactor k and first perform Abel in e over its interval. Both the remaining weight in d and its derivative with respect to log d cost O(E_t L(1+log R)), using the derivative bounds of 946 and the maximum of increments E_t of the opposite side. Now integrate by parts against d(M−c) on (a,J]. The two edges cost O(R E) by the preceding factor and the interior costs ∫_a^J|M−c|dt/t≪R E by a≫y/R. Sum the O(R²) cofactors and absorb polylogarithms: |V(x)|≪L^C E_t E+O(x^(−1)). The floor of 962 for |V(x)| and E_t≪_ε t^(Θ+ε), t=x/J, force E≥y^(Θ−o(1)) in the sense of exponents, uniformly in c. The converse limsup is obtained with c=0 and the classical global bound. This proof uses the smoothness of the bilinear weight; the maximum of 962 alone did not justify the claim about the mean.

For each trimmed set one can restrict the infimum to centers between the minimum and the maximum of M on B_y (projecting to the interval only reduces distances). Thus |M−c|≤O_ε(y^(Θ+ε)) and the loss in the L¹ mean due to the removed set is O_ε(y^(Θ+ε−κ)). The exponent Θ is preserved by choosing ε sufficiently small. For p≥1 apply Hölder in the measure dt/y of mass ≤1. For 0<p<1 use |M−c|≤|M−c|^p (sup |M−c|)^(1−p), and let ε→0. It has not been proved that there exists a band with dispersion at root level.

### In each band, the extreme variance requires positive signed correlation at long distance and with small gcd

Let [l,y]=B_y of 974, of length b=y−l. Its variance under dt/b is exactly
    V_y=Σ_{l<m,n≤y} μ(m)μ(n) K_y(m,n),   K_y=(min(m,n)−l)(y−max(m,n))/b².
Indeed K_y is the covariance of the indicators t≥m,t≥n; the initial value M(l) disappears. By 974, V_y=y^(2Θ+o(1)) at EACH y→∞.

If Θ>1/2, for any α>0 and 0≤h<2Θ−1, the subtotal of the SAME sum restricted to (m,n)≤y^α (gcd) and |m−n|>y^h is also positive and asymptotic to V_y. Thus it is not enough that large sums exist at an isolated scale: in each band the set of distant interactions with small common divisors has to be positive with exponent 2Θ. Here the arithmetic signs and the covariance kernel are preserved. The primitive ratios and their density appear in 059/060; here the sum has not been replaced by its density.

Proof. The near strip, even with an additional condition on the gcd, costs in modulus O(y^(1+h)) by counting and |K_y|≤1. To remove the large gcds it is essential to sum WITH sign. For each d≤y, the sum with d|m,n is a nonnegative variance V_{y,d} and
    V_{y,d} ≪_ε d^ε(y/d)^(2Θ+ε).
If d has a square factor it vanishes. For d squarefree, the process of partial sums over multiples of d is μ(d) times the sum of μ(r) restricted to (r,d)=1 up to t/d. This is expressed exactly as Σ_{s with p|s⇒p|d} M(t/(ds)) by Euler convolution, truncated to argument ≥1. The classical bound for M and the product ∏_{p|d}(1−p^(−Θ−ε/4))^(−1)≪_ε d^(ε/2) give the indicated bound upon squaring (adjust the initial ε). The estimate of the product follows by taking each sufficiently large prime with (1−p^(−Θ−ε/4))^(−1)≤p^(ε/2) and absorbing the others into the constant.

By divisor inversion, the weight 1_{(m,n)>G} is Σ_{d|m,n} c_d, with c_d=Σ_{g|d,g>G}μ(d/g). In particular c_d=0 for d≤G and |c_d|≤τ(d). Summing the bound on V_{y,d} yields O_ε(y^(2Θ+ε) G^(1−2Θ+ε)) for Θ>1/2 and small ε (renaming losses). With G=y^α, 0<α<1, the exponent is strictly smaller than 2Θ for ε sufficiently small; α≥1 is trivial. Removing afterwards the near strip costs another term of strictly smaller exponent. This gives the claimed equivalent. A root ceiling for the dispersion remains unproved; the sign of each correlation is not obtained, nor can G be fixed constant with this proof.

### A prefixed mesh of additive frequencies detects the exponent in each band

With B_y of 974, increasing K once if necessary, define
    P_y(j)=Σ_{n∈B_y} μ(n) exp(2πi j n/y).
Without hypotheses on the zeros,
    log(1+max_{0≤j≤(log y)^4}|P_y(j)|)/log y → Θ.
In particular an unbounded sequence of y with simultaneous ceiling y^(1/2+o(1)) at those frequencies suffices. The mesh depends only on y; its points are all close to the additive frequency zero.

Details of the localization in frequency. For the x of 962 put T=x/y and average the integer cut J=⌊zy⌋ with a fixed smooth density of mass one on 0.55<z<0.9. Thus the indicator d≤J becomes a smooth function χ(d/y), equal to one before 0.55 and zero after 0.9. One can use plateaus with derivatives of order m bounded by (Cm²)^m by integration and translation of the test of 890. In the deduction of 931 one can fix for ALL those J the same interval T/(2R)<q≤2C₂T and the cut e>⌊T/R²⌋. Indeed in the additional upper zone the COMPLETE residual vanishes by inversion, in the omitted lower zone Y≥R, and for e≤T/R² one keeps x/(de)≥R². After that operation, removing d≤y/(4C₂R) by a smooth plateau, equal to zero below y/(8C₂R) and equal to one above y/(4C₂R), costs O(x⁻¹) by the same Poisson since q≤2C₂T. All errors are uniform. Denote by χ_R(s) the product of the two plateaus, supported with ys in B_y.

In the resulting formula fix k≪R² and e with T/(2R)<ek≤2C₂T. Expand in Fourier series of period one the function of s
    F(s,e)=χ_R(s) h_w(T/(sek))=χ_R(s)Σ_j w(j s e k/T).
Extended by zero it is smooth. It satisfies ||∂_s^m F||∞≤C R²(C R m²)^m for m≥1: on the support only O(R²) integers j intervene and if a term or derivative of w is nonzero then jek/T≪1/s≪R. The Leibniz rule gives the same type of bound after the plateaus; ||F||∞≪R². By integration by parts m=⌈log y⌉ times, the sum of moduli of the coefficients of frequencies |j|>(log y)^4 is O(y^(−10)) for large y (R≪log y(log log y)²). One can truncate with error o(1) even summing absolutely over d,e,k and the factor log e, since x≤y^(8/5).

For each retained frequency the coefficient, multiplied by log e, has modulus and derivative with respect to log e bounded by a fixed power of log y, uniformly: differentiating under the integral only applies a logarithmic derivative to w and the preceding count remains valid. Abel in e over its interval thus costs O((log y)^C T^(Θ+ε)) by the classical bound for Mertens, including the endpoints. The factor in d is precisely P_y(j), since F is supported inside B_y and the expansion is uniform over the whole band. Summing the polylogarithmic k,j gives |V(x)|≪(log y)^C T^(Θ+ε) max|P_y(j)|+O(1). Conjugation covers negative j. The floor of 962 and x=yT give the liminf; the converse limsup is Abel directly on B_y at cost O(1+|j|). It was not proved that the mesh has a root ceiling along a sequence; the explicit set of observations that would suffice to bound was reduced.

### Shifting the mesh to parity preserves the exponent; extension by local factors

For the same band format (with K fixed sufficiently large) and 0≤j≤log⁴y, replace the frequency j/y of 1000 by 1/2+j/y. The maximum of the sums thus modulated still has exact exponent Θ in EACH band. Therefore the even-odd contrast, although it suppresses the constant component of the modulation, preserves the global obstruction.

More generally, fix a prime p and write c_p(n)=Σ_{a=1}^{p−1} exp(2πian/p), ν_p(n)=−c_p(n)μ(n). The mesh applied to ν_p has exact exponent Θ if Θ≠log(p−1)/log p. It therefore holds simultaneously as an asymptotic statement for each fixed prime except at most one (the thresholds and K may depend on p); uniformity as p grows is not ensured. In particular for each of those primes at least one of the p−1 meshes shifted to a/p contains an amplitude ≥y^(Θ−o(1)), by the triangle inequality. The individual ceiling for a general center a/p does not follow from the ceiling of the aggregate. At p=2 there is a single mesh and no exception is possible.

Proof. Denote by δ_p the Dirichlet mass at p and by 1 the constant arithmetic function. The exact identity
    ν_p * 1 * (δ₁−δ_p) = δ₁+(p−1)δ_p
follows from ν_p(s)=ζ(s)^(−1)(1+(p−1)p^(−s))/(1−p^(−s)) for Re s>1, or by computation on prime powers. In 920 use w_−(u)=w(u)−w(pu) in the copies summed over d,j and w_+(u)=w(u)+(p−1)w(pu) in the initial reading over q. Replace μ(d) by ν_p(d) only in the first index. The preceding identity gives the same exact cancellation when q>C₂x/J: all active indices satisfy dj≤J, and the extra copy of dilation p does not enlarge the upper support. Both new weights have the two vanishing moments and the same qualitative derivative bounds with constants depending on p. The factorial transfer and the elimination of small e in 931 hold term by term with those weights; |ν_p|≤p. The second index e keeps μ(e). The rounding and the smooth expansion of 1000 apply without change of exponent. For the ceiling of the partial sums of ν_p it suffices to expand the local quotient as 1+pΣ_{l≥1}p^(−ls), applying the classical bound on M at each dilation and summing geometrically.

The initial reading has multiplier W(s)(1+(p−1)p^(−s)). The added factor vanishes only on the line Re s=log(p−1)/log p. If this abscissa differs from Θ, there are uncancelled zeros arbitrarily close to the supremum, even if it is attained. Thus the argument of 962 gives the necessary excursions in each window of powers (under RH for p=2 or for the other non-exceptional p, the mean square of the critical spectrum remains positive). The indicated abscissa increases strictly with p: it is 1+log(1−1/p)/log p, with a negative correction decreasing in modulus. Therefore at most one prime requires additional analysis. This computation uses classical Euler factors; it presupposes neither independence of phases nor bounds for L-functions of characters. It does not provide the simultaneous ceiling along a sequence for the parity mesh.

### Where a root bound is indeed obtained for the shifted meshes: size of the exceptional set

Write P_{y,α}(j)=Σ_{n∈B_y}μ(n)e(n(α+j/y)), e(t)=exp(2πit). Unconditionally for almost every FIXED center α, at EVERY sufficiently large real scale y,
    max_{|j|≤log⁴y}|P_{y,α}(j)| ≪_α √y (log y)^7.
The metric property by itself does not prove the ceiling for α=0 or 1/2. More quantitatively, for 1/2<β≤1 the centers where the limsup of the exponent of those meshes is ≥β form a set of Hausdorff dimension ≤2−2β.

Proof. For N=2^k, write A_N(α)=max_{m≤2N}|Σ_{n≤m}μ(n)e(nα)|. Each prefix decomposes into ≤k+2 dyadic blocks and by Cauchy
    A_N²≤(k+2)Σ_I |Σ_{n∈I}μ(n)e(nα)|²,
with the sum over ALL dyadic blocks contained in [1,2N] of the binary partition. In each generation Σ_IΣ_{n∈I}μ(n)²≤2N. Parseval and Chebyshev give measure O(k^(−4)) for A_N>√N k³. Borel–Cantelli and Abel with the phase e(nj/y) (variation O(1+|j|) on [1,y]) prove the simultaneous ceiling even when both endpoints of the interval vary. This is an elementary orthogonality estimate; it does not depend on the particular bounded sequence being μ.

For the dimensional refinement, on any set of points α_i separated by 1/N one has Σ_i A_N(α_i)²≪N²(k+2)². Indeed for each block polynomial F, integrating over disjoint arcs of radius 1/(3N) centered at the α_i gives Σ_i|F(α_i)|²≪N∫|F|²+N^(−1)∫|F'|²≪N∫|F|² by Cauchy applied to the fundamental theorem of calculus and Parseval. Sum the blocks as before. If for some y∈[N,2N] the mesh exceeds N^b with b<β, Abel implies A_N(α)≫N^b/(k+2)^4. A maximal separated family of those centers has O(N^(2−2b)(k+2)^10) points, and their arcs of radius 1/N cover all bad centers. Summing over dyadic N the diameters raised to s>2−2b and then letting b↑β proves the statement.

Proximity without sufficient rate does not identify the critical center either. For each integer y, discrete Parseval gives Σ_{r mod y}|P_{y,0}(r)|²≤y². Within the first ⌊y/log y⌋ indices fit ≫y/log⁵y disjoint blocks of length 2⌊log⁴y⌋+1. One of them has total energy O(y log⁵y); its center r/y→0 and ALL its mesh has modulus O(√y log^(5/2)y). This is unconditional even if there were an outer zero. The specific obligation remains a ceiling at a center that preserves the detector, such as 0 or 1/2; choosing a favorable center after measuring does not guarantee it.

### A single prescribed length of ordinary increments, averaging over their origins

With L=log y and H=⌊y exp(−√L)⌋, let
    A_H(y)=(1/y) Σ_{r integer, [r+1,r+H]⊂B_y} |M(r+H)−M(r)|.
Then at EACH large y A_H(y)=y^(Θ+o(1)). All intervals have the same length, smaller than y/log^A y for any fixed A. μ is summed without twist and the interval is not filled by periodicity nor with zeros at the edges. This does not follow from 974 alone: a periodic profile of period H could have large dispersion and all those increments zero.

Proof. Use the x and the smooth weights F(d/y,e) of 1000 BEFORE expanding in Fourier. Denote by A the average of the translations by 1,…,H in the integer variable d, and m=⌈5√L⌉. Decompose F=A Σ_{i=0}^{m−1}(I−A)^i F+(I−A)^m F. The second term costs in supremum at most (H/y)^m||∂_s^m F||∞, integrating m ordinary differences of steps ≤H. By 1000 this is ≤y^(−5+o(1)), uniformly in e,k. Even the absolute sum over all d,e,k with log e costs o(1), since x≤y^(8/5). In the first term move the outer A onto μ by change of index; now only an ordinary increment of M of length H divided by H enters. All remaining translations combined have sum of moduli of coefficients ≤2^m and indices shifted by at most mH. The support of F has endpoints ≥y/(8C₂R) and ≤0.9y, with R of 1000, so that upon enlarging the constant K of B_y all increments used lie entirely inside the band: mH=o(y/R).

For each origin and each translation, the weight in the variable e and its derivative in log e are O((log y)^C), as in 1000, independently of the shift in d. Abel over e on its interval, and the sum over k, then give
    |V(x)| ≪ (log y)^C 2^m T^(Θ+ε) (y/H) A_H(y) + O(1),   T=x/y.
Both 2^m and y/H are y^o(1). Applying the floor of 962 at x and letting ε→0 yields the lower exponent Θ. The upper one comes from the classical global bound on M. Consequently a root ceiling along ONE sequence for the mean of those increments of fixed length per scale suffices. The possible loss by resonance with the step H in the exponent has been ruled out, without exhibiting that sequence.

### An outer zero requires reinforcement in a positive proportion of the dyadic lengths

In each band B_y fix a_n=μ(n)1_{n∈B_y} and S_h(r)=Σ_{r<n≤r+h}a_n. Over all integer starts put E_h=Σ_r S_h(r)² and c_h=Σ_r S_h(r)S_h(r+h)/E_h. It is a normalized correlation without subtracting the mean. Let J=⌊log₂(y exp(−√log y))⌋. Then, without hypotheses on the zeros,
    (1/J) Σ_{j=0}^{J−1} log₂(1+c_(2^j)) → 2Θ−1.
In particular if Θ>1/2 and 0<b<2Θ−1, the lower proportion of j in each band with c_(2^j)>2^b−1 is at least (2Θ−1−b)/(1−b). Thus coherent reinforcement between adjacent sums cannot be confined to o(log y) exceptional lengths. It is not required that each individual product have positive sign.

Proof. For H=2^J the bounds of 1042 are kept: changing H by a factor between 1/2 and 1 preserves both the difference error and y/H=y^o(1). Cauchy over the interior intervals gives E_H≥y^(1+2Θ−o(1)); the edge trims only add squares. In the other direction each sum is an increment of M in B_y, O_ε(y^(Θ+ε)), and there are O(y) starts. Moreover E₁=Σ_{n∈B_y} μ(n)²∼6y/π² by the classical density of squarefree numbers. The identity S_(2h)(r)=S_h(r)+S_h(r+h) gives E_(2h)=2E_h(1+c_h). All energies are positive for large y (take an interval ending at the first nonzero entry). By Cauchy c_h≤1 and by the identity c_h>−1. The telescoping product of the J factors is E_H/(H E₁)=y^(2Θ−1+o(1)). Taking logarithms and using J∼log₂ y proves the limit. If a fraction p of the logarithms exceeds b, their mean is at most p+(1−p)b, whence the proportion bound.

This gives another verifiable sufficient obligation: if along some sequence of bands for each τ>0 only o(log y) lengths have c_h>τ, RH follows. The converse implication has not been proved for this counting condition: negative logarithms can compensate positive ones. The exact equivalence corresponds to the logarithmic average tending to zero along a subsequence. The doubling identity by itself does not determine that arithmetic average.

### The frequency detector tolerates erasures chosen after measuring

Let N=⌊log⁴y⌋. In 1000 one can erase any m=m(y) frequencies of {0,…,N} with m log log y=o(log y), even the m of largest modulus. The maximum of the remaining ones is still y^(Θ+o(1)) for each large y. Therefore a sequence with root ceiling except for that number of exceptions suffices. The statement also holds for the parity mesh of 1016.

Proof. Symmetrize the erased indices into a set D⊂[−N,N], |D|≤2m, and use the smooth periodic function F(s,e) of 1000. There is an open arc of length ≍1/R in the complement of B_y/y, close to 0, because the lower endpoint of B_y/y is 1/[K log y(log log y)²] and R≍log y(log log y)². There place a periodic bump ψ of integral 1 with ||ψ^(l)||∞≤C R(CR max(1,l)²)^l, by dilation of the fixed smooth test. For j∈D define
    G_j(s)=e(js) ∏_{k∈D,k≠j}(1−∂_s/[2πi(k−j)]) ψ(s).
In this formula e(js)=exp(2πijs). The Fourier coefficients at the indices of D are exactly δ_jk, by multiplication of the differential symbols. Then F*=F−Σ_{j∈D} F̂(j,e)G_j coincides with F on ALL of B_y/y and has zero coefficients on D.

Cost details. If M=|D|, the sum of moduli of the coefficients of the differential polynomial before modulating is ≤2^M by integer spacing. The demodulated amplitude of G_j has derivative of order l of norm ≤C R 2^M (CR max(1,l+M)²)^(l+M), uniformly in j. For l=0,2 this is y^o(1), so the ℓ¹ norm of its Fourier coefficients is also y^o(1) by two integrations by parts; modulating only shifts the list. For l=⌈log y⌉, integrating l times shows that the ℓ¹ tail at distance >N/2 from j is O(y^(−12)): the quotient CR(l+M)²/N≪(log log y)²/log y and the leftover factor of order M has logarithm o(log y). Therefore the corrections for |j|≤N/2 do not harm the truncation. Those for |j|>N/2 come multiplied by F̂(j,e)=O(y^(−15)), by the derivatives of 1000 with the same l. Even summing their full norms they only cost y^(−15+o(1)). The tail of F satisfies the same estimate. The retained coefficients of F* and their derivatives with respect to log e are y^o(1), since the G_j are independent of e and both F and its derivative in log e have polylogarithmic sup norm. Repeat the Abel of 1000 over e: only y^o(1) is lost and the floor for |V(x)| forces the maximum over surviving indices to have exponent Θ. The preceding upper bound still holds. For parity the same empty arc and the modified weight of 1016 are used.

The erasure does not filter the series of zeros: the weight is interpolated IN THE COMPLEMENT of the support of the samples of μ. In particular the theorem requires numerous large amplitudes if Θ>1/2, but does not yet give an arithmetic upper bound for the remaining ones.

### The squarefree support is straightened at root precision; limit of the local data

Let δ=1/ζ(2), n_r the r-th squarefree integer and ε_r=μ(n_r)∈{−1,1}. In the ORIGINAL mesh of 1000 one can replace the sum by
    Σ_{r integer, r∈δ B_y} ε_r exp(2πi j r/(δy))
with error O((1+|j|)√y), uniform for |j|≤log⁴y. Indeed Q(t)=Σ_{n≤t}μ²(n)=δt+O(√t) by μ²(n)=Σ_{d²|n}μ(d), rounding in d≤√t and the tail of Σ1/d². It follows that n_r=r/δ+O(√r). The two endpoints change O(√y) ranks and each common phase changes by O(|j|/√y). This proves the global equivalence with a root ceiling for the phases on the uniform lattice of ranks, even after the erasures authorized in 1064. If Θ>1/2 the exact exponent is preserved; when Θ=1/2 the transfer bound by itself gives the ceiling, not a floor. The classical count of squarefree numbers is not a new sign route.

Counterexample to identification by support, PNT and finite local laws: for EACH finite set of primes P and each 1/2<β<1 there is a real sequence b(n) of signs on the EXACT support of μ satisfying b(1)=1 and b(pn)=−b(n) if p∈P and p∤n, whose partial sum is c_P x^β+O_P(1+x^(β−1/2)), c_P>0. Take the squarefree m coprime to all primes of P in increasing order. Their count is δ_P t+O_P(√t), δ_P=δ∏_{p∈P}p/(p+1), by the same quadratic inversion and count of integers coprime to the fixed product. At the r-th m assign the sign S_r−S_{r−1}, where S_r is the integer of the parity of r closest to r^β, resolving ties downward, S_0=0. They are always ±1: the roundings differ by an odd integer of modulus <3 for r≥2 because 0<r^β−(r−1)^β<1. The first increment is +1. The sum over these m is δ_P^β t^β+O_P(1+t^(β−1/2)). For n squarefree write n=dm with d a divisor of the product of P, and multiply the sign of m by μ(d). Outside that support put zero. Summing yields c_P=δ_P^β∏_{p∈P}(1−p^(−β))>0. It even coincides with μ up to any prefixed cut upon including all its primes in P. The mean tends to zero with a power saving, while the untwisted reading over B_y is ∼c_P y^β. The example does not satisfy the global multiplicativity required of μ: it shows exactly why the support, any finite prefix and those local identities do not suffice to infer the uniform ceiling. The obligation remains in the actual order of the signs ε_r.

### Sign matching with free endpoints: exact cost and divisibility barrier

In the band [l,y]=B_y of 974 place a token at each squarefree integer of (l,y], with its sign μ. Match opposite tokens at the cost of the ordinary distance; a token can also be sent to either endpoint at the cost of its distance to that endpoint. The total minimum cost is EXACTLY
    C(y)=min_c ∫_l^y |M(t)−c|dt = y^(1+Θ+o(1)).
In particular RH is equivalent to constructing matchings of cost ≤y^(3/2+o(1)) along some sequence y→∞, with no balance condition between the two signs in the band. Any explicit matching can be used for the upper bound, without proving its optimality.

Proof. In the ordered chain of positions including the endpoints, every transport induces a net integer flow in each gap. Conservation at each token forces the flow to be M(t)−c with the same integer constant in all gaps; the cost is at least the sum of lengths times moduli of the flows. Conversely choose an integer median of the values of M under dt (if there is an interval of medians, some endpoint is an integer). Orient and multiply each edge according to that flow. Conservation and integer decomposition into paths give pairs between sources and sinks, including the endpoints. No path crosses the complete band from end to end if a median attained on a gap is chosen: on that gap the flow is zero. Each path is monotone in the chain and its cost is the distance between its endpoints. This gives the equality even for non-integer l and y and tokens of zero cost at an endpoint. The exponent comes from 974. One-dimensional transport via the primitive is classical; here it is used with both endpoints free for the criterion of prescribed bands.

Arithmetic obstruction to constructing those pairs: if each pair a,b must satisfy max(a,b)/(a,b)≤D then the cost is ≫y²/D for D≥1, since for distinct a,b |a−b|≥(a,b)≥max(a,b)/D. There are ≫y tokens in [y/3,2y/3] and sending them to an endpoint costs ≫y per unit; count each pair at most twice. More generally any certificate of cost ≤y^(3/2+o(1)) can use that type of pair with D≤y^(1/2−η), or a dispatch to the endpoints, on only O(y^(1−η+o(1))) tokens of that zone, for each 0<η<1/2. If one directly requires that one integer divide the other, the distance is at least half of the larger and the cost is ≫y² independently of D. The elementary global involution pairing each odd squarefree n with 2n cancels the signs exactly, but does not have the necessary geometry. One would need a much closer matching with large reduced quotients, or an equivalent bound for the optimal transport.

### Effective local matching and stability of the cost of the sparse remainder

Take h=⌈log log y⌉. There is a deterministic construction in each band B_y that matches all but o(y) actual tokens at distances ≤h. If C_res is the minimum of 1084 for the tokens it leaves, one has, unconditionally,
    |C_res−C(y)|≤yh,   C_res/C(y)→1.
Thus all the difficulty at root scale persists in an explicit subset of density zero, even after fixing the short pairs. This is an application of the short-interval theorem, not a new proof of local cancellation. Matching by blocks with mesh shifting was already applied in 195 (index 35959); here the OPTIMAL cost is compared before and after and its equivalent is preserved.

For each mesh origin modulo h, in each complete block match in order until one of the two signs is exhausted. |Σ_block μ| tokens remain. Choose the origin minimizing the remaining number, breaking ties by smallest origin, leaving the two partial edge blocks untouched. The mean of those counts is at most 2h+h^(−1)Σ_r |M(r+h)−M(r)| for the starts in the band. By [Matomäki–Radziwiłł](https://arxiv.org/abs/1501.04585) and trivial bounding, on each interval [X,2X] with X comparable to y, this last sum is o(yh). To cover the whole band apply the result on a fixed number of octaves down to ηy and bound the lower part by ηyh+O(h²); at the end let η→0. The selected mesh has o(y) leftovers and the total cost of the fixed pairs is ≤yh.

For stability use the representation by primitives of 1084: when removing a pair a,b of opposite signs, the primitive changes only on the interval between a and b, by one unit in modulus. By the triangle inequality and optimization over the SAME space of constants, the change of the minimum cost is ≤|a−b|, even if all remaining pairs are reorganized. Sum and use C(y)=y^(1+Θ+o(1)), Θ≥1/2. If the global sign fails, among those o(y) tokens at least y^(Θ−o(1)) remain (the cost of sending each to the nearest endpoint is ≤y). Density zero by itself would only give o(y²), not the required cost y^(3/2+o(1)).

### A single tree of blocks certifies the cost at all distances

In B_y=(l,y] take H a power of two with y<H≤2y and a uniform real shift s∈[0,H). Use the nested meshes s+hℤ for h=1,2,…,H (powers of two only). Define N_h(s)=Σ_I |Σ_{n∈I} μ(n)| over cells of size h entirely interior to the band. For a generic origin there is an explicit matching of cost ≤O(y)+Σ_{h<H}2h N_h(s), and E_s N_h≤2C(y)/h. Therefore at least half of the shifts give a cost ≤O(y+C(y)log y), against the floor C(y) for every matching. The logarithmic bound is the usual one for random partitions; here the free edge is handled and the same origin serves for ALL lengths.

Construction: in an interior cell cancel all available opposite signs before moving up to the parent; exactly the modulus of the original balance of the cell remains. When a parent contains some endpoint of the band, send the tokens left over to an endpoint contained in that parent and remove them. Already in the first generation the tokens of edge cells can be sent, at cost ≤O(y). At each step the number that can move is ≤N_h and each trip costs ≤2h. At H there is no interior cell. For the average compare with an optimal solution of 1084, including the dispatches to endpoints as edges. Each interior cell with absolute balance b has at least b optimal edges leaving it. An edge of length d is cut by the mesh with probability min(1,d/h), and counts in at most two cells. Summing gives E_s N_h≤2C(y)/h, without presupposing any probability distribution for μ. The exceptional shifts with data on a boundary can be omitted; to minimize the certificate one representative per stretch between boundary events suffices.

Equivalent arithmetic criterion: along a sequence y→∞ it suffices to find ONE s per band satisfying simultaneously h N_h(s)≤y^(3/2+o(1)) for each dyadic h between √y and H, with uniform o(1). For h<√y one has N_h≤y+1 and its summed cost is O(y^(3/2)). For the others it suffices to sum O(log y) bounds. Conversely, under RH the optimal cost is y^(3/2+o(1)) by 1084 and the mean estimate of the certificate gives the condition for at least half of the origins. If Θ>1/2, for EACH generic origin some length ≥√y requires h N_h≳C(y)/log y, since the construction produces an admissible transport. The simultaneous bound on the actual balances at large lengths remains open; the tree does not establish it.

### Which zero-free region a single level of the tree would certify

Let 1/2<λ<1 be fixed and h=⌊y^λ⌋ (the immediately lower dyadic also works). For a sequence y→∞, the budget h N_h(s)≤y^(3/2+o(1)) at ONE chosen origin per band suffices to conclude
    Θ≤max(λ, 3/2−λ).
If the budget is proved on AVERAGE over the origins, the stronger result is Θ≤3/2−λ, even when λ>3/4. These are implications; no such budget has been verified outside the trivial regime.

For the first, every subinterval of B_y is divided into complete cells and two remainders of length ≤h (with possible O(1) from endpoints). Its increment of M has modulus ≤N_h(s)+2h+2. Apply the exact local exponent of 962 and the budget, taking logarithms along the sequence. For the second, with uniform integer shift modulo h one has exactly h E_s N_h=Σ_r |M(r+h)−M(r)| over entirely interior windows, with the convention (r,r+h] and endpoints adjusted to include the same windows in 1042. Take H'=h⌊y exp(−√log y)/h⌋, which is ∼y exp(−√log y) for λ<1. The proof of 1042 preserves its exponent for H' by the same difference estimates and inversion cost. Every window of length H' is a union of H'/h windows of length h. Summing their moduli over all interior starts, each short window is used at most H'/h times. Hence
    E_s[h N_h(s)] ≥ (h/H') y^(1+Θ−o(1)) ≥ y^(λ+Θ−o(1))
in EACH sufficiently large band, which gives the implication on average. Changing the conventions at an edge origin is avoided by taking half-open cells with the same endpoints as the windows; the actual average over s coincides up to null sets if the band endpoints are integers and are adjusted by one unit, with no effect on the proof using the strictly interior windows.

For example, λ=0.51 at a single favorable shift per scale along a subsequence would already exclude zeros with real part >0.99. With λ=0.9 the average budget would exclude Reρ>0.6, while the single mesh chosen in this argument only excludes Reρ>0.9. This calibrates the difficulty of the first power jump beyond √y; these are not zero-free regions established here.

### The budget is paid up to √y times any logarithmic power, at all origins

For any fixed A,B>0, in the tree of 1104 the sum of costs up to length √y(log y)^A is O_{A,B}(y^(3/2)/(log y)^B), uniformly in the shift. At the last complete level of that range, the number of tokens remaining in the interior cells is O_D(y/(log y)^D) for every fixed D. These are unconditional bounds for y sufficiently large depending on the exponents.

Use the published maximal estimate of Matomäki–Radziwiłł–Shao–Tao–Teräväinen, Theorem 1.1(i), already used in 195:5552. For X^(1/3+1/30)≤2h≤X, the integral over u∈[X,2X] of the maximum of |Σ_{n∈I}μ(n)| over subintervals I⊂(u,u+2h] is O_D(Xh/log^D X): use the constant function and apply the trivial bound on the exception. The published maximum even includes progressions. [Primary source](https://doi.org/10.1007/s00222-026-01408-6).

For a cell (r,r+h] of ANY mesh, that same cell is contained in (u,u+2h] for all u∈[r−h,r]. These integration stretches are disjoint except at endpoints when traversing the mesh. Therefore N_h(s) is bounded by 1/h times the maximal integral over [l−h,y]. For y^(2/5)≤h≤√y(log y)^A, l≈y/[K log y(log log y)²] is much larger than h. In all the octaves covering the integration interval the hypotheses of the theorem hold and log X∼log y. Summing by length one obtains N_h(s)≪_D y/log^D y for every mesh. When summing 2h N_h over dyadic lengths in this range a geometric series up to the largest h suffices. Choose D>A+B. For h<y^(2/5) the trivial total cost is O(y^(7/5)), sufficient even with the announced logarithmic saving.

The maximal argument avoids having to select different good origins depending on the length. The saving in the number of tokens beats any fixed inverse power of log y, but does not provide a fixed power saving in y. In particular √y(log y)^A is still smaller than y^0.51 eventually. The conditional zero-free region of 1112 does not follow.

### Orthogonal separation: only squares of coarse contrasts in a prefixed partition remain

Take B_y=[l,y], b=y−l, and divide exactly into halves starting from the complete interval. For each parent I of length h with halves I_−,I_+ put D_I=mean_{I_+} M−mean_{I_−} M. For H=√y(log y)^A, A fixed positive, and any B>0 one has UNCONDITIONALLY
    ∫_{B_y}|M−mean_{B_y}M|² dt = Σ_{I: |I|>H} (|I|/4) D_I² + O_{A,B}(y²/log^B y).
All retained terms are nonnegative. D_I is the sum of μ(n) with a triangular weight that equals zero at the endpoints of the parent and one at its midpoint: it arises from integrating the indicator t≥n over each half. No origin nor fitting center is chosen. The variance identity is the classical Haar decomposition; the saving in the remainder for these lengths uses the arithmetic maximal estimate of 1124.

Indeed when splitting an interval the unnormalized variance is distributed into the variances of the two halves plus (|I|/4)D_I². Stop simultaneously at leaves of equal length h₀∈(H/2,H]. In each leaf its variance is ≤h₀ U_I², where U_I is the largest modulus of an increment of M between two points of the leaf. By the same integration over enveloping windows of length 2h₀ of 1124, Σ_leaves U_I≪_D y/log^D y for every fixed D: the maximal estimate also controls any subinterval of the leaf. Moreover U_I≤h₀+1. Thus the total remainder costs O_D(y h₀²/log^D y); choose D>2A+B. This proof does not infer a quadratic bound from an L¹ transport without controlling the large values.

By 974 with p=2, the left side has exponent 1+2Θ. If Θ>1/2, the coarse sum constitutes its equivalent in EACH band. In both directions, RH is equivalent to a sequence where that single sum of squares is ≤y^(2+o(1)). There are O(√y/log^A y) contrasts. A uniform bound y^(2+o(1)) for the sum of each level also suffices, since there are only O(log y) levels. The arithmetic bound for those coarse contrasts has not been proved; the missing cancellations must occur inside the triangular sums, before squaring.

### Coprime Liouville pairs: exact scope of grouping by greatest common divisor

Let L(t)=Σ_{n≤t}λ(n), E(y)=y⁻¹∫₀ʸL(t)²dt and P(y)=Σ_{(m,n)=1}λ(mn)(1−max(m,n)/y)₊ over positive ordered pairs. Expanding the square and extracting d=gcd(m,n), complete multiplicativity eliminates λ(d)² and gives E(y)=Σ_{d≤y}P(y/d). Möbius inversion gives P(y)=Σ_{d≤y}μ(d)E(y/d).

The upper bound P(y)≤Cε y^(1+ε) for each ε>0 would suffice. Indeed E(y)≤Cε ζ(1+ε)y^(1+ε). On [X,2X] Cauchy–Schwarz gives ∫|L(t)|t^(−σ−1)dt≪X^(1/2−σ+ε/2). Summing dyadically, s∫₁^∞ L(t)t^(−s−1)dt continues ζ(2s)/ζ(s) holomorphically to Re s>1/2. The numerator does not vanish there, which excludes outer zeros. Conversely under RH the classical Mertens bound and L(t)=Σ_{d≤√t}M(t/d²) give L(t)≪ε t^(1/2+ε); adjusting ε and inverting one even obtains |P(y)|≪ε y^(1+ε).

Elementary obstruction: replacing each summand by its modulus costs Θ(y²). For N=⌊y/2⌋ at least (2−ζ(2))N² pairs of the square [1,N]² are coprime (exclude common multiples by summing over d≥2); the weight is at least 1/2. The exact grouping eliminates the common divisor but leaves the cancellation within the coprime pairs to be proved. Classification: reformulation and discard of the absolute majorant, with no window or sign advance. The identity was checked with exact fractions at the 128 half-integers and integers from 1 to 64.5.

### Obstruction to multiplicative matching within classes of coprime pairs

In the sum P(y) above, fix 0<c<1 and allow an arbitrary set S_y of primes ≤cy. Group the pairs according to both integers remaining after removing all their factors in S_y. Let B_r be the total signed contribution of each class r. Even if ALL internal cancellation is carried out before estimating, Σ_r max(B_r,0) ≫_c y²/(log y)². This rules out the strategy of paying the positive classes and discarding the negative ones, even when the set of allowed primes grows linearly with y.

Proof. Choose fixed max(c,1/2)<a<b<1. For distinct primes p,q in (ay,by) the class of (p,q) has a single pair with nonzero weight: p,q are not removed and any of their proper multiples exceeds y. Its sign is λ(pq)=+1 and its weight is ≥1−b. By the prime number theorem the interval contains (b−a+o(1))y/log y primes; with distinct ordered pairs one gets the bound. The result also holds for any finer partition. An estimate of P at scale y^(1+ε) by this method requires compensating contributions between classes. A lower bound of the same order for P, which retains both signs, does not follow.

### Absorption of the terminal stretch of primes of length y^(2/3)

Let h=y^(2/3), z=y−h and C(y) the sum P(y) restricted to pairs both of whose coordinates have all their prime factors ≤z (1 admitted). For y>8 one has z>y/2. For z≤t≤y let r(t) be the number of primes in (z,t]. The only excluded coordinates are those same primes. The sum of λ over the remaining coordinates up to t is L(t)+r(t), and each excluded prime is coprime to all of them. Ordered pairs with exactly one excluded coordinate contribute −2r(L+r); those with two contribute r(r−1). Integrating the count up to t reproduces the triangular weight, so that
    P(y)−C(y)=−(1/y)∫_z^y [2r(t)L(t)+r(t)²+r(t)]dt.
By r≤h+1 and Cauchy–Schwarz one gets |P−C| ≪ h^(3/2)√(E(y)/y)+h³/y ≪ √(y E(y))+y.

Therefore it suffices to prove the one-sided inequality C(y)≤Kε y^(1+ε) for all ε>0. Indeed for X≥1 let M_X=sup_{1≤u≤X} E(u)/u^(1+ε), finite by elementary bounding. For u≤X the identity gives P(u)≤(Kε+O(1)+O(√M_X))u^(1+ε), enlarging the constant on u≤8. Summing over u=y/d for y≤X and dividing by y^(1+ε) gives M_X ≤ ζ(1+ε)(Kε+O(1)+O(√M_X)). Solving this quadratic inequality bounds M_X independently of X. The Mellin implication of 1144 concludes RH. Under RH, the preceding bounds for E,P conversely give |C(y)|≪ε y^(1+ε).

The absorption allows the rows and columns of the terminal primes to be eliminated collectively even with a moving cut. The estimate of C remains pending. The exponent 2/3 is the one this argument admits with r≤h+1, without claiming optimality.

### The exponent 2/3 is optimal for the uncorrected trimming, under RH

For h=y^α with fixed 2/3<α<1, let C_α be the same restricted sum of 1158. Under RH there are arbitrarily large y with C_α(y) ≫_α y^(3α−1)/(log y)². In particular the uniform target C_α(y)≤Kε y^(1+ε) for all ε>0 itself would fail under RH. This settles the question of optimality in power exponent for this specific trimming.

Indeed, partition [X,2X] into ⌈4X^(1−α)⌉ disjoint cells of equal length. By the prime number theorem one cell, with right endpoint v, contains ≫X^α/log X primes. Take y=v+X^α/2; since y^α≥X^α, the whole cell lies after z=y−y^α. On [v,y] the counter r of 1158 retains at least those primes. Then ∫r²/y ≫ X^(3α−1)/(log X)². In the exact identity C_α=P+∫(r²+2rL+r)/y use 2rL≥−r²/2−2L². The cost of ∫L²/y is at most E(y); under RH both E(y) and |P(y)| are Oε(y^(1+ε)) by 1144. Choosing 0<ε<3α−2 gives the conclusion, with y comparable to X. The majorant r≤h+1 and Cauchy–Schwarz likewise give |C_α|≪ε y^(3α−1+ε) under RH. The power of the positive excursions is thus determined. The dominant term comes from the square of the number of extracted primes.

### Scope and limit of smoothing the terminal cut

The index already records flat weights in 213-047 and 213-159. We audit whether changing only the weight removes the obstruction of 1168. In P replace the triangular weight by w(v)=exp(1−1/(1−v)) for 0≤v<1, zero for v≥1, with v=max(m,n)/y. Denote the coprime sum by P_w and its restricted version by C_w. The sum over all pairs E_w(y)=∫₀¹(−w′(u))L(yu)²du is Σ_d P_w(y/d). Since −w′ is bounded and has a positive minimum on [0,1/2], one has c E(y/2)≤E_w(y)≤C E(y). By the same sum and inversion of 1144, the upper bound of order y^(1+ε) for P_w, for all ε>0, is equivalent to RH.

For z=y−y/log y every pair with some coordinate >z has weight ≤e/y; together they cost at most e y in absolute value, even omitting composite integers. In particular the equivalence holds for C_w with terminal primes removed. The additional length comes entirely from the weight.

For any proportional cut z=cy with fixed 1/2<c<1, on the other hand, under RH C_w itself reaches order ≥const_c y²/log² y for all sufficiently large y. Indeed, in the identity of 1158 now integrate against (−w′(u))du, with r counting the primes in (cy,uy] for u≥c. On a fixed interval [a,b] interior to (c,1), r≫_c y/log y by the prime number theorem (choose a,b depending on c), and −w′ has a positive minimum. Completing the square as in 1168, C_w≥P_w+½∫(−w′)r²−2E_w. Under RH both possible errors cost Oε(y^(1+ε)). The same argument applies to every decreasing C¹ weight with w(1)=0, strictly negative derivative on (0,1) and positive minimum of −w′ on [0,1/2]. This distinguishes enlarging the negligible stretch from arithmetic control on a fixed fraction of the scale.

### Rank compression of the prime rows on a fixed fraction

Fix 1/2<c<1 and k=⌊√y⌋. Order the primes of (cy,y] and group them k at a time. Replace the k labels of each complete block by k distinct labels located at the last prime of the block. Omit the last incomplete block. Each label has value −1; two prime labels are compatible exactly when they are distinct, even if they share a position. They are compatible with every retained coordinate outside the set of terminal primes, since their only positive multiples ≤y are themselves. Outside those primes the original coprimality test is kept. Call Q(y) the ordered sum thus obtained with the original triangular weight. A single representative per block suffices: the internal pairs of that block contribute k(k−1) times the weight at its position, and two distinct blocks have multiplicity k².

For each prefix up to t let r be the original number of terminal primes and s=k⌊r/k⌋ the number of represented labels, δ=r−s. The contribution with both coordinates outside the band of primes is preserved, the mixed one changes by 2δ(L(t)+r), and the internal one by s(s−1)−r(r−1). Consequently
    Q(y)−P(y)=y⁻¹∫₀ʸ [2δ(t)L(t)+δ(t)²+δ(t)]dt.
Since 0≤δ<k, |Q−P|≤2k√E(y)+k²+k. The absorption in 1158 and its converse apply literally: RH is equivalent to Q(y)≤Kε y^(1+ε) for all ε>0. For fixed c, by the prime number theorem O_c(√y/log y) representative prime positions in the band suffice instead of order y/log y. Distinct labels at the same position are necessary to keep the pairs with distinct primes. This preserves the mixed terms and allows deterministic compression on a fixed fraction, without establishing the bound on Q.

### Limit of compression with arbitrary representatives in the prime band

In the label model of 1182, under RH, for 0<η<1/2 and every sufficiently large integer y, any choice of m≤y^(1/2−η) representatives in (cy,y], even with multiplicities chosen after seeing all the arithmetic, produces Q(y) ≫_{c,η} y^(1+2η)/(log y)³. The power 1/2 of the number of necessary positions is determined within this model, up to subpower factors.

Proof. The represented counter s(t) takes at most m+1 values. Let p₁<…<p_N be the primes of the band. On each open interval (p_j,p_j+1) of length one, for j<N, the true counter r equals j. Let b_i be the possible values of s. Then ∫(r−s)² dt ≥ Σ_{j=1}^{N−1} min_i(j−b_i)². Assign each integer to the nearest b_i. For d distinct integers the sum of squares with respect to any real center is at least d(d²−1)/12: it is minimized at the mean, and the variance identity via pairwise differences gives the minimum when the integers are consecutive. By convexity of d³, the preceding sum is at least ((N−1)³/(m+1)²−(N−1))/12. This even admits real centers and adaptive assignment. Since N is of order y/log y by the prime number theorem, D=y⁻¹∫(r−s)²dt ≫ y^(1+2η)/(log y)³.

The identity of 1182 remains exact for general multiplicities with the same internal count s(s−1). Completing the square, δ²+(2L+1)δ≥δ²/2−(2L+1)²/2, hence Q≥P+D/2−O(E+1). Under RH, |P|+E≪ε y^(1+ε); take ε<2η. Thus optimizing the location or the multiplicities of a few representatives does not avoid the positive excess produced by the quadratic discrepancy.

### The three contributions of the fixed prime band, without assuming RH

Let B be the set of primes in (cy,y], with c fixed in (1/2,1), and A=y⁻¹∫₀ʸ r(t)²dt with r(t)=#(B∩[1,t]). Split P(y) by the number i=0,1,2 of coordinates in B, and denote their contributions by U_i. Then U₂=A+O(y), U₁=−2A+O_D(y²/log^D y) and U₀=A+O_D(y²/log^D y) for each fixed D. Moreover A∼(1−c)³ y²/(3log²y). In particular the three signs and the proportion 1:−2:1 are unconditional for y sufficiently large.

Indeed, U₂=avg(r²−r), U₁=−2avg(r²+rL), where avg is the normalized integral on [0,y]. The classical Mertens bound coming from the zero-free region gives |M(t)|≪_D t/log^D(2t), and L(t)=Σ_{j≤√t}M(t/j²) gives the same bound for L (separate j≤t^(1/4) and the trivial tail). Thus avg(rL)≪_D y²/log^D y, E(y)≪_D y²/log^D y and P(y) satisfies that same absolute bound by the inversion of 1144: for d≤√y use log(2y/d)≥½log y, and for the rest sum O(y²/d²). Subtracting U₁ and U₂ from P gives U₀. Finally the prime number theorem gives r(yu)=(u−c)y/log y+o_c(y/log y) uniformly in c≤u≤1; integrating the square yields the constant.

This computation determines how the band interacts with its complement to the precision of any inverse power of the logarithm, relative to the exact profile A. It is a consequence of classical estimates, with no new exponent gain for P. The residual sum must be controlled up to y^(1+ε) to obtain the sign.

### A prime perturbation of free amplitude per band, with absorbable loss

Fix J and partition (y/2,y] into J equal intervals. In each interval j use its first 2k primes with k=⌊N_j/2⌋. Perturb their values λ(p)=−1 by adding a on the first k and subtracting a on the following k, with a real; the other coordinates remain intact. Let P_j(a) be the triangular coprime sum with the modified values in both coordinates. Define H_J(y)=max_j inf_a P_j(a), requiring y sufficiently large depending on J. For each ε>0 the upper bound H_J(y)≪ε y^(1+ε) with a single integer J>8ζ(1+ε) suffices. Conversely RH gives that bound for every fixed J.

Let b_p=±1 be the perturbation without the amplitude and let h(t)=Σ_{p≤t}b_p, v(t)=Σ_{p≤t}b_p². Since no perturbed prime has a proper multiple ≤y, incrementing the sum over all pairs and subtracting the change of the prime diagonal gives
    P_j(a)=P+2a avg[h(L+1)]+a² avg[h²−v].
The profile h vanishes outside its interval. For fixed J and large y, avg h² ≫_J (y/log y)²: by the prime number theorem on the interior quarters of the interval, the number of primes up to a point of the central third lies between (1/4)N_j and (3/4)N_j uniformly, and h there is ≫N_j. On the other hand avg v≤y. The curvature is therefore at least avg(h²)/2. Minimizing the quadratic and applying Cauchy gives 0≤P−inf_a P_j(a)≤2 avg[1_j(L+1)²]. By disjointness of the J bands, P−H_J≤4(E+1)/J.

In the supremum M_X of 1158 sum the last inequality over dilations; absorb (4/J)ζ(1+ε)M_X. Arguments below the curvature threshold are treated directly by bounding P on a compact set. Thus E(y)≪ε y^(1+ε). The converse follows from H_J≤P. It is a relaxation of the criterion: in each band the most favorable amplitude can be chosen, without bounding it or requiring the same amplitude across bands. There is not yet an upper bound for those minima. The maximum over bands is essential for this proof.

### A single minimum with simultaneous perturbations in the terminal band

For y>e² allow real changes b_p at all primes p>y−y/log y up to y. Write H(t)=Σ_{p≤t}b_p and V(t)=Σ_{p≤t}b_p². Admit b if avg V≤½ avg H² (includes zero). Let D(y) be the infimum of the perturbed triangular coprime sum over those vectors; at smaller scales use P. The condition D(y)≪ε y^(1+ε), only as an upper bound, for each ε>0, is equivalent to RH. There is a single minimization per scale and the admission condition is verified without knowing L.

Proof. As in 1206, the exact difference is avg[H²−V+2H(L+1)]. Completing the square, 0≤P−D≤2 avg_terminal (L+1)². Fix 1<s<2 and allow c<1 to be chosen arbitrarily close to 1. For y sufficiently large, the band is contained in (cy,y]; thus P(y)≤D(y)+4[E(y)−cE(cy)]+4. Extend E by zero below 1 and enlarge the constant on the initial compact (P and D are locally bounded by the same inequality of squares).
